Picture: If your class owns a rented locker key, you must decide what happens when someone copies the key (rent a new locker? share?), moves it (hand over), or throws it away (return the locker). If you own nothing special, let the compiler worry.
RuleStatement
Rule of ZeroBest: use members that manage themselves (vector, string, unique_ptr) and write no special member functions
Rule of Three (C++98)If you need a destructor, copy constructor or copy assignment, you probably need all three
Rule of Five (C++11)...plus move constructor and move assignment
A hand-written dynamic array with deep copy, move, and every special member traced (run for real)

#include <algorithm>
#include <iostream>
#include <utility>

// A class that owns raw memory must define copy AND move properly (the Rule of Five).
class Buffer {
    int* data_ = nullptr;
    std::size_t size_ = 0;
public:
    explicit Buffer(std::size_t n = 0) : data_(n ? new int[n]() : nullptr), size_(n) { std::cout << "  construct (" << size_ << ")\n"; }
    ~Buffer() { delete[] data_; std::cout << "  destroy (" << size_ << ")\n"; }

    Buffer(const Buffer& o) : data_(o.size_ ? new int[o.size_] : nullptr), size_(o.size_) {          // 1) copy constructor: DEEP copy
        std::copy(o.data_, o.data_ + size_, data_); std::cout << "  deep copy (" << size_ << ")\n"; }
    Buffer& operator=(const Buffer& o) {                                                             // 2) copy assignment
        if (this != &o) { Buffer tmp(o); swap(tmp); }                                                 // copy-and-swap: safe even if copying throws
        std::cout << "  copy assign\n"; return *this; }
    Buffer(Buffer&& o) noexcept : data_(std::exchange(o.data_, nullptr)), size_(std::exchange(o.size_, 0)) {   // 3) move constructor: steal
        std::cout << "  move (stole pointer)\n"; }
    Buffer& operator=(Buffer&& o) noexcept {                                                         // 4) move assignment
        if (this != &o) { delete[] data_; data_ = std::exchange(o.data_, nullptr); size_ = std::exchange(o.size_, 0); }
        std::cout << "  move assign\n"; return *this; }
    void swap(Buffer& o) noexcept { std::swap(data_, o.data_); std::swap(size_, o.size_); }
    std::size_t size() const { return size_; }
    int& operator[](std::size_t i) { return data_[i]; }
};

int main() {
    std::cout << "create a:\n"; Buffer a(3); a[0] = 7;
    std::cout << "copy b = a:\n"; Buffer b = a;
    std::cout << "change b[0], a unaffected (deep copy): "; b[0] = 99; std::cout << "a[0]=" << a[0] << " b[0]=" << b[0] << '\n';
    std::cout << "move c = std::move(a):\n"; Buffer c = std::move(a);
    std::cout << "a.size() after move = " << a.size() << ", c.size() = " << c.size() << '\n';
    std::cout << "copy assign b = c:\n"; b = c;
    std::cout << "move assign from a temporary:\n"; c = Buffer(5);
    std::cout << "end of main:\n";
}

// ----- Output (real run: Clang 22 + libc++, C++23) -----
// create a:
//   construct (3)
// copy b = a:
//   deep copy (3)
// change b[0], a unaffected (deep copy): a[0]=7 b[0]=99
// move c = std::move(a):
//   move (stole pointer)
// a.size() after move = 0, c.size() = 3
// copy assign b = c:
//   deep copy (3)
//   destroy (3)
//   copy assign
// move assign from a temporary:
//   construct (5)
//   move assign
//   destroy (0)
// end of main:
//   destroy (5)
//   destroy (3)
//   destroy (0)

Copying made an independent deep copy (changing b[0] left a alone); the move constructor stole the pointer and left a with size 0; assignment operators and destructors ran at the expected moments. Every construct was matched by a destroy.

Good to know

  • Write = default or = delete explicitly to document intent (a non-copyable class: T(const T&) = delete;).
  • Copy-and-swap is a neat way to write a safe assignment operator.

52 Composition, Aggregation and Association

Picture: A car owns its engine (they are born and die together: composition). A team uses players who exist without the team (aggregation). A teacher knows a student (association).
Fig S10 — Composition (filled diamond) vs aggregation (hollow diamond) vs plain association.
Owned parts, borrowed parts and an injected dependency (run for real)

#include <iostream>
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

// COMPOSITION: the whole creates and owns its parts (a Car has an Engine; they live and die together)
class Engine {
public:
    Engine() { std::cout << "  Engine built\n"; }
    ~Engine() { std::cout << "  Engine destroyed\n"; }
    void start() const { std::cout << "  engine running\n"; }
};
class Car {
    Engine engine_;                               // member object: part of the car
    std::string model_;
public:
    explicit Car(std::string m) : model_(std::move(m)) { std::cout << "  Car " << model_ << " built\n"; }
    ~Car() { std::cout << "  Car " << model_ << " destroyed\n"; }
    void drive() const { engine_.start(); std::cout << "  " << model_ << " is moving\n"; }
};

// AGGREGATION: the whole only REFERS to parts that exist independently (a Team has Players)
class Player { public: std::string name; explicit Player(std::string n) : name(std::move(n)) {} };
class Team {
    std::vector<const Player*> members_;          // non-owning pointers
public:
    void add(const Player& p) { members_.push_back(&p); }
    void list() const { for (auto* p : members_) std::cout << "  " << p->name; std::cout << '\n'; }
};

// DEPENDENCY: uses another type only briefly (as a parameter)
struct Printer { void print(const Player& p) const { std::cout << "  printing player " << p.name << '\n'; } };

// Prefer COMPOSITION to inheritance: build behaviour by plugging parts in
struct Logger { virtual ~Logger() = default; virtual void log(const std::string& m) const = 0; };
struct ConsoleLogger : Logger { void log(const std::string& m) const override { std::cout << "  [console] " << m << '\n'; } };
class Service {
    std::unique_ptr<Logger> logger_;               // swap the logger without touching Service
public:
    explicit Service(std::unique_ptr<Logger> l) : logger_(std::move(l)) {}
    void run() const { logger_->log("service running"); }
};

int main() {
    std::cout << "composition:\n";
    { Car c("Zephyr"); c.drive(); }
    std::cout << "aggregation (players outlive the team):\n";
    Player p1("Asha"), p2("Ravi");
    { Team t; t.add(p1); t.add(p2); t.list(); }
    std::cout << "  players still exist: " << p1.name << ", " << p2.name << '\n';
    Printer{}.print(p1);
    std::cout << "has-a with an injected part:\n";
    Service s(std::make_unique<ConsoleLogger>()); s.run();
}

// ----- Output (real run: Clang 22 + libc++, C++23) -----
// composition:
//   Engine built
//   Car Zephyr built
//   engine running
//   Zephyr is moving
//   Car Zephyr destroyed
//   Engine destroyed
// aggregation (players outlive the team):
//   Asha  Ravi
//   players still exist: Asha, Ravi
//   printing player Asha
// has-a with an injected part:
//   [console] service running

The engine was built before the car body finished and destroyed after it (composition). The players survived the team (aggregation). The service accepted a logger from outside (dependency injection), so tests can swap in a fake.

Good to know

  • Prefer composition over inheritance: it is more flexible and keeps classes independent.
  • In C++ composition is a member object; aggregation is a pointer, reference or shared_ptr to something owned elsewhere.

53 Casts and RTTI

Picture: Showing your ID at a door: a cast asks 'treat this as another type', but a careful guard (the right cast) checks whether that is allowed.
CastUseSafety
static_cast<T>Ordinary conversions, base↔derived when you are sureCompile-time checked
dynamic_cast<T*>Safe downcast in a polymorphic hierarchy; returns nullptr (or throws bad_cast for references) on failureRun-time checked (needs RTTI and a virtual function)
const_castAdd or remove constOnly for legacy APIs; modifying a truly const object is UB
reinterpret_castReinterpret raw bits/pointersDangerous
std::bit_cast (C++20)Reinterpret an object's bytes as another typeSafe, constexpr
C-style (T)xAny of the above silentlyAvoid
Each cast in action, plus typeid and endianness (run for real)

#include <bit>
#include <cstdint>
#include <iostream>
#include <string>
#include <typeinfo>

struct Animal { virtual ~Animal() = default; };
struct Dog : Animal { void bark() const { std::cout << "  Woof!\n"; } };
struct Cat : Animal {};

void legacy_print(char* s) { std::cout << "  legacy says: " << s << '\n'; }

int main() {
    // static_cast: ordinary, checked-at-compile-time conversions
    double pi = 3.99; int whole = static_cast<int>(pi);
    std::cout << "static_cast<int>(3.99) = " << whole << '\n';

    // dynamic_cast: safe down-casting using run-time type info (needs a virtual function in the base)
    Animal* pet = new Dog;
    if (Dog* d = dynamic_cast<Dog*>(pet)) { std::cout << "pet is a Dog:\n"; d->bark(); }
    if (Cat* c = dynamic_cast<Cat*>(pet)) { (void)c; } else std::cout << "pet is not a Cat (dynamic_cast gave nullptr)\n";
    try { Animal& ref = *pet; (void)dynamic_cast<Cat&>(ref); } catch (const std::bad_cast&) { std::cout << "reference version throws bad_cast\n"; }
    std::cout << "typeid(*pet).name() mentions Dog? " << (std::string(typeid(*pet).name()).find("Dog") != std::string::npos) << '\n';
    delete pet;

    // const_cast: remove const (only OK if the object was not originally const)
    char text[] = "mutable text";
    const char* cp = text;
    legacy_print(const_cast<char*>(cp));

    // reinterpret_cast: tell the compiler to treat bits differently (dangerous). std::bit_cast is the safe copy-the-bits version.
    float f = 1.0f;
    auto bits = std::bit_cast<std::uint32_t>(f);
    std::cout << "bit_cast<uint32_t>(1.0f) = 0x" << std::hex << bits << std::dec << '\n';
    int n = 0x01020304;
    auto* bytes = reinterpret_cast<unsigned char*>(&n);          // inspecting bytes through unsigned char is allowed
    std::cout << "first byte of 0x01020304 is " << +bytes[0] << " -> this machine is " << (bytes[0] == 4 ? "little" : "big") << "-endian\n";

    // C-style cast (T)x does whichever of the above compiles: avoid it
    std::cout << "Prefer: static_cast > dynamic_cast > const_cast > reinterpret_cast; avoid C-style casts.\n";
}

// ----- Output (real run: Clang 22 + libc++, C++23) -----
// static_cast<int>(3.99) = 3
// pet is a Dog:
//   Woof!
// pet is not a Cat (dynamic_cast gave nullptr)
// reference version throws bad_cast
// typeid(*pet).name() mentions Dog? 1
//   legacy says: mutable text
// bit_cast<uint32_t>(1.0f) = 0x3f800000
// first byte of 0x01020304 is 4 -> this machine is little-endian
// Prefer: static_cast > dynamic_cast > const_cast > reinterpret_cast; avoid C-style casts.

static_cast<int>(3.99) gave 3; dynamic_cast found the Dog but returned nullptr for a Cat and threw for the reference form; bit_cast showed the bits of 1.0f (0x3f800000); the first byte of 0x01020304 was 4, so this machine is little-endian.

Good to know

  • Needing many dynamic_casts usually signals a design problem: add a virtual function instead.

54 Design Patterns in Modern C++

Picture: Recipes that other cooks discovered: you do not have to invent a way to make sure there is only one oven, or to let many people watch the same news channel.
PatternIdeaModern C++ way
SingletonExactly one instanceA function-local static (thread-safe since C++11)
FactoryCreate objects without naming the concrete classA function returning std::unique_ptr<Base>
ObserverNotify subscribers of eventsA list of std::function callbacks
StrategySwap an algorithm at run timeA std::function or lambda member, or a template parameter
PimplHide implementation details behind a pointer to speed up compilesstd::unique_ptr<Impl>
RAII guards, CRTP, Visitor (std::visit), Builder, Decorator, Adapter ...See the LLD guide for all 23 GoF patternslow-level-design.html
Singleton, factory, observer, strategy and pimpl in modern C++ (run for real)

#include <functional>
#include <iostream>
#include <map>
#include <memory>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

// 1) Singleton (Meyers): one instance, created on first use, thread-safe since C++11
class Config {
    Config() { std::cout << "  Config created once\n"; }
public:
    Config(const Config&) = delete; Config& operator=(const Config&) = delete;
    static Config& instance() { static Config c; return c; }
    std::string app = "demo";
};

// 2) Factory: create objects without naming the concrete class
struct Transport { virtual ~Transport() = default; virtual std::string go() const = 0; };
struct Car : Transport { std::string go() const override { return "driving"; } };
struct Boat : Transport { std::string go() const override { return "sailing"; } };
std::unique_ptr<Transport> make(const std::string& kind) {
    static const std::map<std::string, std::function<std::unique_ptr<Transport>()>> registry{
        {"car", [] { return std::make_unique<Car>(); }}, {"boat", [] { return std::make_unique<Boat>(); }}};
    auto it = registry.find(kind); return it == registry.end() ? nullptr : it->second();
}

// 3) Observer: subscribers get notified
class EventBus {
    std::vector<std::function<void(const std::string&)>> subs_;
public:
    void subscribe(std::function<void(const std::string&)> f) { subs_.push_back(std::move(f)); }
    void publish(const std::string& m) const { for (auto& f : subs_) f(m); }
};

// 4) Strategy: swap the algorithm
struct Pricing { virtual ~Pricing() = default; virtual double price(double base) const = 0; };
struct Regular : Pricing { double price(double b) const override { return b; } };
struct Sale : Pricing { double price(double b) const override { return b * 0.8; } };
class Cart { std::unique_ptr<Pricing> p_; public: explicit Cart(std::unique_ptr<Pricing> p) : p_(std::move(p)) {} double total(double base) const { return p_->price(base); } void set(std::unique_ptr<Pricing> p) { p_ = std::move(p); } };

// 5) PIMPL idiom (shown in one file): hide implementation details behind a pointer
class Widget {
    struct Impl { int secret = 42; int compute() const { return secret * 2; } };
    std::unique_ptr<Impl> impl_ = std::make_unique<Impl>();
public:
    int value() const { return impl_->compute(); }
};

int main() {
    Config::instance().app = "changed"; std::cout << "same instance? " << std::boolalpha << (&Config::instance() == &Config::instance()) << " app=" << Config::instance().app << '\n';
    for (const char* k : {"car", "boat", "plane"}) { auto t = make(k); std::cout << k << ": " << (t ? t->go() : "unknown kind") << '\n'; }
    EventBus bus; int heard = 0;
    bus.subscribe([&](const std::string& m) { ++heard; std::cout << "  subscriber A got: " << m << '\n'; });
    bus.subscribe([&](const std::string& m) { ++heard; std::cout << "  subscriber B got: " << m << '\n'; });
    bus.publish("order placed"); std::cout << "messages delivered: " << heard << '\n';
    Cart cart(std::make_unique<Regular>()); std::cout << "regular: " << cart.total(100) << ", ";
    cart.set(std::make_unique<Sale>()); std::cout << "sale: " << cart.total(100) << '\n';
    std::cout << "pimpl value: " << Widget{}.value() << '\n';
}

// ----- Output (real run: Clang 22 + libc++, C++23) -----
//   Config created once
// same instance? true app=changed
// car: driving
// boat: sailing
// plane: unknown kind
//   subscriber A got: order placed
//   subscriber B got: order placed
// messages delivered: 2
// regular: 100, sale: 80
// pimpl value: 84

One shared config instance was proved identical by address; the factory returned a car, a boat and a plane (and an 'unknown kind' fallback); two subscribers were notified of one event; two pricing strategies gave 100 and 80; the pimpl hid the implementation yet still computed 84.

Good to know

  • Use patterns as a vocabulary, not a checklist. Most are much shorter in modern C++ thanks to lambdas, std::function and smart pointers.

55 SOLID and Good Class Design

Picture: Rules for tidy toy boxes: one kind of toy per box (single responsibility), add new toys without rebuilding the box (open/closed), and so on.
PrincipleIn one lineC++ hint
Single ResponsibilityA class should have one reason to changeSmall classes; split I/O from logic
Open/ClosedOpen for extension, closed for modificationNew derived classes / strategies instead of editing an if chain
Liskov SubstitutionA derived object must work wherever the base is expectedDo not weaken guarantees in overrides
Interface SegregationMany small interfaces beat one fat oneSmall abstract classes / concepts
Dependency InversionDepend on abstractions, not concrete classesPass interfaces by reference/unique_ptr (dependency injection)

Good to know

  • Full explanations with violating-vs-fixed examples (in Python, but the ideas are identical) are in the System Design (LLD) guide.
  • Also: Law of Demeter (talk only to your friends), DRY, YAGNI.

56 Project: A Small Banking System (OOP Together)

Picture: Putting every OOP idea in one small project: an abstract base, two account types, a manager, error handling and smart pointers.
Fig S11 — The project: an abstract Account with Savings and Current accounts; Bank owns the accounts.
The bank: openings, an overdraft error, an unknown account, transfers and month-end interest (run for real)

#include <iomanip>
#include <iostream>
#include <map>
#include <memory>
#include <stdexcept>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

// A bigger OOP example: accounts with inheritance + polymorphism + exceptions + STL containers
class Account {
protected:
    std::string id_, owner_;
    double balance_;
    std::vector<std::string> log_;
public:
    Account(std::string id, std::string owner, double opening) : id_(std::move(id)), owner_(std::move(owner)), balance_(opening) {}
    virtual ~Account() = default;
    virtual void withdraw(double amt) {
        if (amt <= 0) throw std::invalid_argument("amount must be positive");
        if (amt > balance_) throw std::runtime_error("insufficient funds in " + id_);
        balance_ -= amt; log_.push_back("withdraw " + std::to_string(amt));
    }
    void deposit(double amt) { if (amt <= 0) throw std::invalid_argument("amount must be positive"); balance_ += amt; log_.push_back("deposit " + std::to_string(amt)); }
    virtual void month_end() = 0;                                  // each kind of account does something different
    virtual std::string kind() const = 0;
    double balance() const { return balance_; }
    const std::string& id() const { return id_; }
    const std::string& owner() const { return owner_; }
};

class Savings : public Account {
    double rate_;
public:
    Savings(std::string id, std::string o, double bal, double rate) : Account(std::move(id), std::move(o), bal), rate_(rate) {}
    void month_end() override { double interest = balance_ * rate_ / 12; deposit(interest); }
    std::string kind() const override { return "Savings"; }
};

class Current : public Account {
    double overdraft_;
public:
    Current(std::string id, std::string o, double bal, double overdraft) : Account(std::move(id), std::move(o), bal), overdraft_(overdraft) {}
    void withdraw(double amt) override {                           // overrides the rule: may go below zero up to the overdraft
        if (amt <= 0) throw std::invalid_argument("amount must be positive");
        if (amt > balance_ + overdraft_) throw std::runtime_error("overdraft limit exceeded in " + id_);
        balance_ -= amt; log_.push_back("withdraw " + std::to_string(amt));
    }
    void month_end() override { if (balance_ < 0) balance_ -= 10; }   // fee when overdrawn
    std::string kind() const override { return "Current"; }
};

class Bank {
    std::map<std::string, std::unique_ptr<Account>> accounts_;
public:
    Account& open(std::unique_ptr<Account> a) { auto& slot = accounts_[a->id()]; slot = std::move(a); return *slot; }
    Account& get(const std::string& id) { auto it = accounts_.find(id); if (it == accounts_.end()) throw std::out_of_range("no account " + id); return *it->second; }
    void transfer(const std::string& from, const std::string& to, double amt) { get(to); get(from).withdraw(amt); get(to).deposit(amt); }   // check the target exists first
    void month_end() { for (auto& [id, a] : accounts_) a->month_end(); }
    void report() const {
        std::cout << std::fixed << std::setprecision(2);
        for (const auto& [id, a] : accounts_) std::cout << "  " << std::left << std::setw(6) << id << std::setw(9) << a->kind() << std::setw(8) << a->owner() << std::right << std::setw(10) << a->balance() << '\n';
    }
};

int main() {
    Bank bank;
    bank.open(std::make_unique<Savings>("S-1", "Asha", 1000, 0.06));
    bank.open(std::make_unique<Current>("C-1", "Ravi", 200, 300));
    std::cout << "opening balances:\n"; bank.report();
    bank.transfer("S-1", "C-1", 250);                                // C-1 now has 450
    try { bank.get("C-1").withdraw(900); } catch (const std::exception& e) { std::cout << "error: " << e.what() << '\n'; }   // 900 > 450 + 300
    bank.get("C-1").withdraw(700);                                    // 700 <= 750: allowed, balance becomes -250
    try { bank.transfer("S-1", "X-9", 10); } catch (const std::exception& e) { std::cout << "error: " << e.what() << '\n'; }
    bank.month_end();
    std::cout << "after transfers and month-end:\n"; bank.report();
}

// ----- Output (real run: Clang 22 + libc++, C++23) -----
// opening balances:
//   C-1   Current  Ravi        200.00
//   S-1   Savings  Asha       1000.00
// error: overdraft limit exceeded in C-1
// error: no account X-9
// after transfers and month-end:
//   C-1   Current  Ravi       -260.00
//   S-1   Savings  Asha        753.75

Account types behave differently through one interface: the current account allowed an overdraft up to its limit and rejected more; the savings account earned interest at month-end (the balance moved from 1000.00 to 753.75 after transfers and interest). Errors were reported with exceptions.

Good to know

  • Try extending it: add a statement history (a std::vector of transactions), file saving, or a 'fixed deposit' account type.

57 OOP Pitfalls in C++

Picture: Common potholes on the road: knowing them is half of avoiding them.
PitfallWhy it hurtsFix
Missing virtual destructor in a polymorphic baseUB, leaksvirtual ~Base() = default;
SlicingDerived behaviour lostPass by reference/pointer; use smart pointers
Forgetting overrideTypo silently creates a new functionAlways write override
Rule of Three/Five violationsDouble free, shallow copiesRule of Zero; = delete
Calling virtual functions in constructorsBase version runsUse an init() or factory
Deep inheritance treesFragile, hard to readPrefer composition, keep hierarchies shallow
Public data members everywhereInvariants cannot be enforcedPrivate data + behaviour methods
Exposing internals with T& gettersCallers break invariantsReturn copies/const references
Raw owning pointers as membersLeaks and double deletesunique_ptr / values
Non-explicit single-argument constructorsSurprising implicit conversionsUse explicit

Part D — The STL (Standard Template Library) in Detail

58 What Is the STL? The Big Picture

Picture: A well-stocked toolbox. Containers are the boxes (vector, map...), algorithms are the tools (sort, find...), and iterators are the hands that let any tool reach into any box.
Fig S12 — The three parts of the STL: containers hold data, algorithms work on it, and iterators connect the two.
FamilyMembersHeader(s)
Sequence containersvector, array, deque, list, forward_list, (string)<vector> <array> <deque> <list> <forward_list>
Ordered associativeset, multiset, map, multimap<set> <map>
Unordered (hash)unordered_set/map and multi versions<unordered_set> <unordered_map>
Flat (C++23)flat_map, flat_set: sorted vectors with a map interface<flat_map> <flat_set> (library support varies)
Adaptorsstack, queue, priority_queue<stack> <queue>
Viewsstring_view, span, mdspan (C++23)<string_view> <span> <mdspan>
Utilitiespair, tuple, bitset, optional, variant<utility> <tuple> <bitset>
Algorithms and numericssort, find, accumulate, ...<algorithm> <numeric> <ranges>

Good to know

  • The STL is the part of the standard library designed by Alexander Stepanov: generic programming: one sort works for every container that offers random access.
  • The rest of this part tours every container, every iterator kind, the main algorithms, function objects, ranges, utilities, allocators, performance and how to choose.

Java Collections ↔ C++ STL ↔ Python: Side by Side

Three kitchens with the same tools under different names. A whisk is a whisk everywhere; only the label on the drawer changes. This table tells you which drawer to open in each language. You are in the C++ guide, so the middle column is the one you already know best. See also the same table in the Java guide and the Python guide.

Read one row at a time: the first column says what you need, the next three say what it is called in Java (java.util), C++ (STL, <vector>, <map> and friends) and Python (built-ins, collections, heapq, bisect, queue), the last column is the trap to remember.

You need...JavaC++ (STL)PythonWatch out
Part 1 — Sequences
Growable array (the default list)ArrayList (interface List)std::vectorlistIndex access is O(1) in all three; insert/remove in the middle is O(n). Java stores objects (boxing for numbers), C++ stores the values themselves side by side, Python stores references and may mix types.
Fixed-size arrayint[], helper class Arraysstd::array, C arrayarray.array (typed numbers), tuple (fixed and read-only)Python has no true fixed-length array in the core; NumPy arrays (third-party) are the usual choice for numbers.
Doubly linked listLinkedListstd::list (std::forward_list = singly linked)none in the library: collections.deque is the closest, or write your own node classLinked lists lose to arrays for almost everything because of cache misses; all three communities say 'start with the array'.
Double-ended queueArrayDeque (interface Deque)std::dequecollections.dequePython list.pop(0) is O(n); use deque.popleft() (O(1)) for queues.
Stack (LIFO)ArrayDeque with push/pop (old Stack class is legacy)std::stack (an adapter over deque)list with append/popJava Stack extends the old synchronized Vector: avoid it. C++ pop() returns nothing; read top() first.
Queue (FIFO)Queue implemented by ArrayDeque or LinkedListstd::queuecollections.deque (queue.Queue is the thread-safe one)queue.Queue uses locks and blocks; it is slower than deque in a single thread.
Priority queue (heap)PriorityQueue (min-heap)std::priority_queue (max-heap by default)heapq functions on a plain list (min-heap)Easy to trip on: C++ gives the largest first, Java and Python the smallest. For a C++ min-heap add std::greater<int>. Python has no max-heap; push negated values.
Part 2 — Maps and sets
Hash map (key → value)HashMapstd::unordered_mapdictAverage O(1) lookup in all three. Java/C++ order is not guaranteed; Python dict keeps insertion order (guaranteed since 3.7).
Map that remembers insertion orderLinkedHashMapnone (unordered_map has no order; keep a vector of keys)dict (already does it)LinkedHashMap with access order is the classic LRU-cache building block; Python uses collections.OrderedDict for that.
Sorted map (keys in order)TreeMapstd::mapnone in the standard library; sortedcontainers.SortedDict (third-party) or keep a sorted list with bisectRed-black tree, O(log n). Gives 'smallest key', 'floor/ceiling' (floorKey, lower_bound).
Hash set (unique items)HashSetstd::unordered_setsetElements/keys must be hashable: a Python list cannot be a key (use a tuple); in Java/C++ define equality + hash for your own type.
Set that remembers insertion orderLinkedHashSetnonedict.fromkeys(items) (keys used as an ordered set)Handy for 'remove duplicates but keep order'.
Sorted setTreeSetstd::setnone in the standard library; sortedcontainers.SortedSet (third-party) or bisectO(log n) insert and search.
Duplicates allowed, sortedno standard class (use TreeMap<K,Integer> counts)std::multiset, std::multimapcollections.Counter (counts) or defaultdict(list)Java keeps counts in a map; C++ stores each duplicate separately.
Count how often each item appearsmap.merge(k, 1, Integer::sum)++m[k] on unordered_map<T,int>collections.CounterCounter returns 0 for a missing key and has most_common(n).
Map with a default for missing keysgetOrDefault, computeIfAbsentoperator[] (creates a default value)collections.defaultdict, dict.get(k, default)Missing key: Java get gives null, C++ at throws ([] inserts), Python d[k] raises KeyError.
Immutable set / listSet.of, List.of, Map.ofconst containersfrozenset, tupleJava's Set.of rejects duplicates and null; Python frozenset can be a dict key.
Part 3 — Special-purpose containers
Bit flagsBitSetstd::bitset, vector<bool>an int with bit operators, or bytearrayPython ints are unlimited in size, so one int can act as a bitset of any length.
Pair / small recordrecord, Map.Entrystd::pair, std::tuple, structtuple, typing.NamedTuple, @dataclassTuples/records are the standard way to return several values.
Maybe a valueOptional<T>std::optional<T>None (hint with T | None)Python has no wrapper type: the value itself is checked against None.
Map keyed by an enumEnumMaparray indexed by the enumdictFast and compact in Java because the key is a small integer inside.
Weak referencesWeakHashMapnone in map (use std::weak_ptr)weakref.WeakValueDictionaryEntry disappears when nothing else uses the object.
Thread-safe collectionsConcurrentHashMap, CopyOnWriteArrayList, BlockingQueueno standard thread-safe containers: guard with std::mutexqueue.Queue, LifoQueue, PriorityQueuePlain dict/list operations are safe under the usual CPython GIL (free-threaded builds differ), but compound steps (check-then-set) never are.
Building a long stringStringBuilderstd::string with += or ostringstreamlist of parts + ''.join(parts)Repeated + on Java/Python strings creates a new string each time.

The same job, spelled three ways. (c = any collection, lst/list/v = list-like, m/map/d = map.)

Every method spelling in the second table was compiled and run once (Java 25, g++ 15.2 with C++23, Python 3.14, 16 Nov 2026); the first table is a mapping of concepts.
